Per-voice conditioned-speech corpus — demo grid

Reference voice k325_age3_bg1 (Velvet Sage Baritone, Male, Late 40s to 60s), best DNSMOS variant cbx. Every row shows the rank-0 candidate of that group out of 200, under Profile A (documented best practice) and Profile B (identical, plus laion/moss-mediathek-emotion-lora r64_e2 merged at 25 %).

What am I looking at?

The one-sentence version. We take a single voice, and make it say the same sentence hundreds of different ways — angry, quietly angry, exhausted, breathless, whispering, like a sports commentator, like a dragon — in English and German, and keep everything so it can be used as training data.

Why. The goal is a speech-to-speech translation model that keeps the speaker and the emotion. If you translate a frightened German sentence into English, the output should still be the same person and still sound frightened — not a neutral narrator. To train that you need matched pairs: same meaning, same voice, same emotional intensity, two languages. That is exactly what one row of this grid is.

How it was made, step by step

  1. Pick the voice. Each reference voice exists in three versions (original, enhanced, and converted-to-itself); we keep whichever scores best on an automatic audio-quality metric. The clip at the top of this page is that reference — everything below is trying to sound like that person.
  2. Pick the sentence. Sentences come from a translation corpus, so every English sentence has an exact German counterpart. Nothing longer than 80 words, and spread across many subject areas so the corpus is not all news or all fiction.
  3. Set the condition. A condition is "which emotion, how strongly, held back or let out, which language" — or "which voice property, pushed how far". Each condition has its own recipe: a specific LoRA adapter, a specific merge strength, and specific wording in the prompt. These recipes were measured beforehand, not guessed.
  4. Generate many candidates. The model is asked the same thing 200 times (32 in production). Speech generation is random, so some attempts land the emotion and some do not.
  5. Score every candidate automatically — how strongly the target emotion comes through, how genuine rather than performed it sounds, how cleanly any gasp or sob blends into the speech, how closely it still matches the reference speaker, and whether the words are intelligible. The best one is shown here; all the others are kept too, so the ranking can be changed later without regenerating anything.

The vocabulary on this page

termwhat it means
groupOne condition in one language. All the candidates for it compete against each other; the winner is displayed.
A / B / C / DThe four emotion settings. A = intense and freely expressed, B = moderate and freely expressed, C = intense but contained (strong feeling held back, leaking through), D = moderate and quietly controlled. A and C share one sentence, B and D share one, so the four are directly comparable.
λ (lambda)How strongly the emotion adapter is mixed into the model. Too low and the emotion does not appear; too high and the voice stops sounding like a person. The value differs per emotion and was measured.
spk / speaker simHow much the generated clip still sounds like the reference speaker, 0–1. Do not read this as a percentage of "correct". Among clips a listener confirms are the same person, the median is only about 0.63 — so 0.6 is normal, not bad.
genuinenessWhether the emotion sounds felt rather than acted.
blendWhether a gasp, sob or laugh merges into the speech instead of sounding pasted on.
WERWord error rate — whether the intended words actually came out.

Which groups exist — 832 per voice

blockwhat variesgroups
Emotions40 emotions (affection, anger, awe, grief, relief, shame, triumph …) × the four A/B/C/D settings × English and German320
Voice dimensions57 measurable properties of a voice (tempo, brightness, chest resonance, breathiness, tension, disfluency …) each pushed to four levels — extremely low, moderately low, moderately high, very high — × two languages456
Edge casesthe hard non-verbal things: screams of fear and pain, groans, shivering with cold, laughter, whimpering, crying × two languages28
Sports commentatorthe high-energy commentary style × two languages2
Explicitnessadult delivery × two languages — skipped entirely for any reference voice that reads as underage, checked against three separate age signals2
Character clusters12 character-voice adapters (chesty and low, bright and light, gravelly, breathy …) so this speaker can play an ogre, a dragon or a fae while still being recognisably themselves × two languages. Each is also rendered a second time through voice conversion so the two can be compared.24
total per voice832

What this demo actually is, and what it is not

This page is one voice, generated twice over: once with the standard recipe and once with an extra adapter mixed in at 25 %, to see whether that adapter helps. It is a proof that the whole matrix runs end to end — 323,200 generations, 152 GPU-hours, 0.1 % failures.

The honest caveats. Every score here comes from automatic models, not human listeners, and those models agree with a human only moderately. The four-level ladder for the voice dimensions is our own construction. And the biggest open problem is visible in the numbers: German conditioning holds the speaker much less well than English (0.58 versus 0.38 on average) — which matters a great deal, because German–English pairing is the entire point.

Safety gate: Empathic-Insight Age 3.10 (≥ 2.0), VoiceNet AGEV 4.72 (≥ 2.40), card age 'Late 40s to 60s' → explicitness group ALLOWED (passed)
